\end{overpic}

Figure 5:
This is a (conceptual) spherical image, every curve lies on a great circle. Left: A coupling
(Q_{1},F_{1},Q_{2},0)
and its spherical reflection
(Q_{2}^{\prime},-F_{1},Q_{1}^{\prime},0)
. Right: According to Fig.
3
, the positions of adjacent quads should be upside down, so in order to get a spherical linkage, we need to flip
Q_{2}^{\prime}
respect to
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}
and
Q_{1}^{\prime}
respect to
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
.

Here is another example that shows the existence of the flexible mesh whose spherical linkage contains two arbitrarily coupled quads in Fig.
3
left, i.e. the matching that contains a coupling of an arbitrary class.

Example 3.8

(Reflection)
.

The main idea is extending a coupling of an arbitrary class to a matching. Notice that in table (
3
), the class of the coupling is defined by its components, and the cases of components, given in Definition
3.5
, are independent of
H^{(2)}
, which means the class of a coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
only depends on its first three polynomials. Now suppose we have a coupling in which
g^{(1)},H^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are arbitrary. Set the coefficients of
g^{(3)},g^{(4)}
and
F_{2},F_{3},F_{4}
as follows

(a_{3},b_{3},c_{3},e_{3})=(a_{2},c_{2},b_{2},e_{2}),\,\,(a_{4},b_{4},c_{4},e_{4})=(a_{1},c_{1},b_{1},e_{1}),\,\,F_{2}=F_{4}=0,\,\,F_{3}=-F_{1}.

In this regard
Z(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)})
is just a ’copy’ of
Z(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)})
thus, by Proposition
2.15
, the fiber product
Z(S_{1})\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}Z(S_{2})\cong Z(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)})
is always an infinite set. Recall the paragraph above Section
2.2.2
, the geometric illustration of this example is simply the (spherical) reflection of a given coupling
(Q_{1},F_{1},Q_{2},F_{2}=0)
, see Fig.
5
. This trick actually works in all situations (including (anti)deltoids).

4
Pseudo-planar type without (anti)deltoids

4.1
Section outline and classification theorem

We start with the simplest meshes in which all
F_{i}\in\{0,\infty\}
. In such a type we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\tilde{g}^{(i)}(x_{i},x_{i+1})=g^{(i)}(x_{i},x_{i+1})=a_{i}x_{i}^{2}x_{i+1}^{2}+b_{i}x_{i}^{2}+c_{i}x_{i+1}^{2}+x_{i}x_{i+1}+e_{i}\text{ if $F_{i}=0$,}\\
\tilde{g}^{(i)}(x_{i},x_{i+1})=-b_{i}x_{i}^{2}x_{i+1}^{2}-a_{i}x_{i}^{2}-e_{i}x_{i+1}^{2}+x_{i}x_{i+1}-c_{i}\text{ if $F_{i}=\infty$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

All equations are exactly in the same format as in the planar type, the only difference might be switching the coefficients and that is why we call it
pseudo-planar type
.
9
9

9

In Fig.
3
left, planar type requires
\zeta_{1}=\tau_{1}=0
but pseudo-planar type only requires
\sin(\zeta_{1}+\tau_{1})=0
.
It is easy to check that the coefficients, when
F_{i}=\infty
, also fit the inequalities of Definition
2.6
and the non-sigularity of
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
remains. In this regard, we only need to develop the theory for
F_{i}=0
and directly apply it to
F_{i}=\infty
after switching the coefficients. Hence in this section, we regard
y_{i}=x_{i+1}
and simply write a coupling as
S=(g^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2}),g^{(2)}(x_{2},x_{3}))
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
. According to Definition
3.6
, each class of
S
is equivalent to one of the corresponding conditions. i.e.
S
is

\bullet

purely-rational
if both
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are reducible;

\bullet

half-quadratic
if only one of
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
is reducible;

Further, when both
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible,
S
is

\bullet

involutive-rational
if

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}\text{ and }\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\};

(12)

\bullet

rational-quadratic
if only one of systems (
12
) holds;

\bullet

purely-quadratic
if one of the following systems holds

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left\{\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}\neq 1\right\}\text{ or}\\
\left\{\frac{4a_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{4c_{2}e_{2}},b_{1}e_{1}=a_{2}b_{2}=0\right\}\text{ or}\\
\left\{\frac{4b_{1}e_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{4a_{2}b_{2}},a_{1}c_{1}=c_{2}e_{2}=0\right\};\end{array}\right.

(13)

\bullet

involutive-quadratic
if

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}=\frac{c_{1}}{e_{1}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{e_{2}},\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}\neq\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}\right\};

(14)

\bullet

quartic
if otherwise.

The proof has been split into cases which will be detailed studied.

4.2
Case-by-case study

4.2.1
Purely-rational

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
Since all
g^{(i)}
are reducible, by Proposition
2.15
, each component has the form

W=Z(f^{(1)},f^{(2)},r_{12},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})

where
f^{(1)},f^{(2)}
are irreducible factors of
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
respectively and
r_{12}=\text{Res}(f^{(1)},f^{(2)};x_{2})
. Further, by Lemma
2.10
all extensions are trivial in the extension diagram of
W
, which implies
[K_{1}(x_{3}):K_{1}]=[K_{1}(x_{2}):K_{1}]=1
. Finally, according to Definition
3.6
we know
S
is purely-rational.
∎

4.2.2
Half-quadratic

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
Consider the extension diagram of the component of
S
. If
g^{(1)}
is reducible but
g^{(2)}
is not, we have the left-hand side of the following diagrams

\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{3}(x_{1},x_{2})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{(2)}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{3}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{(1)}}
\textstyle{K_{3}(x_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{(1)}}
\textstyle{K_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{(2)}}

where
f^{(1)}
is an irreducible factor of
g^{(1)}
; or if
g^{(2)}
is reducible but
g^{(1)}
is not, change the parameter to
x_{3}
we have the right-hand side of the above diagrams, where
f^{(2)}
is an irreducible factor of
g^{(2)}
. Each of the diagrams implies a component of
Case 2
which are the only ones possessed by half-quadratic couplings according to Definition
3.6
.
∎

Example 4.2
.

Let us construct a matching in which both couplings
(g^{(1)},g^{(2)}),(g^{(3)},g^{(4)})
respectively admit components
W_{1},W_{2}
of
Case 2
. First, according to Lemma
2.10
we may set
a_{1}=e_{1}=0
and
b_{3}=c_{3}=0
, then set
g^{(2)}
irreducible. To build a matching we need
W_{1}\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}W_{2}
to be an infinite set. On the other hand, in the extension diagrams of
W_{1},W_{2}
we have
x_{2}=kx_{1}
and
x_{4}=\frac{k^{\prime}}{x_{3}}
. Plugging them into
g^{(2)}
and
g^{(4)}
(
g^{(4)}
is undetermined yet) respectively should give two multiples of the same minimal polynomial of
x_{3}
on
K_{1}
according to Theorem
3.3
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(k^{2}a_{2}x_{1}^{2}+c_{2})x_{3}^{2}+kx_{1}x_{3}+(k^{2}b_{2}x_{1}^{2}+e_{2}),\\
(c_{4}x_{1}^{2}+e_{4})x_{3}^{2}+k^{\prime}x_{1}x_{3}+(k^{\prime 2}a_{4}x_{1}^{2}+k^{\prime 2}b_{4}),\\
\end{array}\right.

which should be equal up to a constant, i.e. the coefficients of
g^{(4)}
are determined by

\frac{k^{2}a_{2}}{c_{4}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{4}}=\frac{k}{k^{\prime}}=\frac{k^{2}b_{2}}{k^{\prime 2}a_{4}}=\frac{e_{2}}{k^{\prime 2}b_{4}}.

4.2.3
Involutive-rational

Lemma 4.3
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
where all
g^{(i)}
are irreducible, set
R(x_{1},x_{3})=\text{Res}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})
we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&(x_{3}-kx_{1})^{2}|R(x_{1},x_{3})\Leftrightarrow(x_{3}-kx_{1})|R(x_{1},x_{3})\Leftrightarrow\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}=k;\\
(2)&(x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime})^{2}|R(x_{1},x_{3})\Leftrightarrow(x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime})|R(x_{1},x_{3})\Leftrightarrow\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}.\\
\end{array}

Corollary 4.4
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
where all
g^{(i)}
are irreducible,
S
admits a component of
Case 3
if and only if one of systems (
12
) holds if and only if

\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}=1.

(15)

Proof.

We first prove the equivalence of the last two statements. It is easy to check that either of systems (
12
) implies equation (
15
). Conversely, since
S
is non-singular, we may assume
a_{2}e_{2}\neq 0
(or
b_{2}c_{2}\neq 0
otherwise). By substitution
\left\{b_{2}=\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}},c_{2}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{e_{2}}\right\}
, equation (
15
) implies (one of) systems (
12
), i.e.

\{a_{1}c_{1}=a_{2}b_{2},b_{1}e_{1}=c_{2}e_{2},(a_{2}e_{2}-a_{1}e_{1})(a_{2}e_{2}-b_{1}c_{1})=0\}.

Now suppose
S
admits a component
W
of
Case 3
. In the extension diagram of
W
,
g^{(1)}(x_{1},t)\in K_{1}[t]
determines the minimal polynomial of
x_{2}
on
K_{1}
since
g^{(1)}
is irreducible. By Definition
3.5
,
x_{3}
lies in
K_{1}
hence
x_{3}=f/g
where
f,g\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1}]
and
\gcd(f,g)=1
.
g^{(2)}(t,x_{3})
, as well, determines the minimal polynomial of
x_{2}
on
K_{1}
so the ratios of the corresponding coefficients in
g^{(1)}(x_{1},t)
and
g^{(2)}(t,x_{3})
should coincide, i.e.

\frac{a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1}}{a_{2}x_{3}^{2}+b_{2}}=\frac{x_{1}}{x_{3}}=\frac{b_{1}x_{1}^{2}+e_{1}}{c_{2}x_{3}^{2}+e_{2}}\Leftrightarrow\frac{a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1}}{a_{2}f^{2}+b_{2}g^{2}}=\frac{x_{1}}{fg}=\frac{b_{1}x_{1}^{2}+e_{1}}{c_{2}f^{2}+e_{2}g^{2}}.

(16)

According to Corollary
2.11
, for
i=1,2
,
\{a_{i},...,e_{i}\}
contains at most one zero, hence
\gcd(a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1},x_{1},b_{1}x_{1}^{2}+e_{1})=1=\gcd(a_{2}f^{2}+b_{2}g^{2},fg,c_{2}f^{2}+e_{2}g^{2})
. This implies the second ratio chain in equation (
16
) is a constant in
\mathbb{C}
(i.e.
fg/x_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
) so it is either
x_{3}=kx_{1}
or
x_{3}=k^{\prime}/x_{1}
for some nonzero constant
k
or
k^{\prime}
. Plugging them into equation (
16
) we obtain

\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}=k\text{ or }\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}.

The converse is directly from Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
Given
g^{(i)}
non-singular and

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}=k,\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}\right\}

we know
kk^{\prime}\neq 0
hence
\gcd(x_{3}-kx_{1},x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime})=1
. Apply Lemma
4.3
we have
(x_{3}-kx_{1})^{2}(x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime})^{2}|R(x_{1},x_{3})=\text{Res}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})
. Notice that
R(x_{1},x_{3})
is originally a 4 by 4 determinant with the degree of
x_{1}
at most 4, so
R(x_{1},x_{3})
only has two different factors
(x_{3}-kx_{1})
and
(x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime})
. Consequently, the extension diagram of each component always implies
x_{3}\in K_{1}
(i.e.
Case 3
) hence
S
is involutive-rational.
∎

Example 4.5
.

Let us consider an ideal
M=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},g^{(3)},g^{(4)})
with all
g^{(i)}
irreducible and the coefficients satisfy

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}=\frac{a_{4}}{c_{3}}=\frac{c_{4}}{e_{3}}=\frac{a_{3}}{b_{4}}=\frac{b_{3}}{e_{4}}\right\}

where
k^{\prime}\neq 0
is a given parameter. Clearly, according to Lemma
4.3
, both couplings
(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
and
(g^{(3)},g^{(4)})
admit components of
Case 3
with the extension diagrams in which
x_{3}x_{1}-k^{\prime}=0
. Thus
M
is a matching according to Theorem
3.3
.

4.2.4
Rational-quadratic

The next lemma characterizes equimodular couplings in two aspects.
Proposition
2.16
is crucial for its proof!

Lemma 4.6
.

Given a coupling
S=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
where all
g^{(i)}
are irreducible.
S
is equimodular if and only if in every extension diagram of its components, one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(1)

x_{3}\in K_{1}(x_{2})
; (2)
x_{3}\in K_{2}(x_{1})
; (3)
x_{1}\in K_{2}(x_{3})
; (4)
x_{1}\in K_{3}(x_{2})
.

On the other hand,
S
is equimodular if and only if one of the following systems holds.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left\{\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}\right\}\text{ or}\\
\left\{\frac{4a_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{4c_{2}e_{2}},b_{1}e_{1}=a_{2}b_{2}=0\right\}\text{ or}\\
\left\{\frac{4b_{1}e_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{4a_{2}b_{2}},a_{1}c_{1}=c_{2}e_{2}=0\right\}.\end{array}\right.

(17)

Proof.

In the extension diagram, one should realize that
K_{i}(x_{i+1}),K_{i+1}(x_{i})
are just two representations of the same quotient field of the domain
\mathbb{C}[x_{i},x_{i+1}]/(g^{(i)})
, given
g^{(i)}
is prime. This immediately shows
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
and
(3)\Leftrightarrow(4)
. Now suppose
x_{3}\in K_{2}(x_{1})
so there exist
f_{1},g_{1}\in K_{2}
such that
x_{3}=f_{1}+g_{1}x_{1}
. Due to the irreducibility of
g^{(2)}
,
x_{3}\notin K_{2}
hence
g_{1}\neq 0
, which implies
x_{1}=(x_{3}-f_{1})/g_{1}\in K_{2}(x_{3})
. This trick can show
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
. Next, if we treat
g^{(1)}(t,x_{2})
and
g^{(2)}(x_{2},t)
as polynomials in
K_{2}[t]
, condition (2) means their discriminants are equal up to a square in
K_{2}
. Let
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
be the corresponding discriminants of
g^{(1)}
and
g^{(2)}
respectively. In particular,
\Delta_{1}
is not a square since
g^{(1)}
is irreducible. Hence

\Delta_{1}=-4a_{1}c_{1}x_{2}^{4}+(1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1})x_{2}^{2}-4b_{1}e_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}-4a_{1}c_{1}(x_{2}^{2}-\xi)(x_{2}^{2}-\eta)\text{ if }a_{1}c_{1}\neq 0,\\
(1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1})(x_{2}^{2}-\rho)\text{ if }a_{1}c_{1}=0\\
\end{array}\right.

where
\xi,\eta,\rho\in\mathbb{C}
and
\xi\neq\eta
. So the only square factor that
\Delta_{1}
might have is
x_{2}^{2}
(i.e.
b_{1}e_{1}=0
). All in all, systems (
17
) are just saying
\frac{\Delta_{1}}{\Delta_{2}}
is a square in
K_{2}
so formally
K_{2}(x_{1})=K_{2}(\sqrt{\Delta_{1}})=K_{2}(\sqrt{\Delta_{2}})=K_{2}(x_{3})
hence systems (
17
)
\Leftrightarrow
condition (2). Finally, when all
g^{(i)}
are irreducible, by Definition
3.5
it is easy to see that components of
Case 3
and
Case 4
can be characterized by their common property
K_{1}(x_{2},x_{3})=K_{1}(x_{2})
, i.e. condition (1)
\Leftrightarrow S
is equimodular.
∎

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
Since we have already proved Theorem
4.1
for involutive-rational class, together with Corollary
4.4
we can conclude that, if
S
admits components of
Case 3
and
Case 4
, then only one of systems (
12
) holds. Conversely, suppose
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}(=k)\right\}
holds but
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}
fails. According to Corollary
4.4
we know
S
admits a component of
Case 3
. Symbolic computation can show

(a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},e_{2})=\left(\frac{a_{1}}{k},kc_{1},\frac{b_{1}}{k},ke_{1}\right)\Rightarrow\frac{\text{Res}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})}{(x_{3}-kx_{1})^{2}}=\left(\frac{a_{1}b_{1}}{k^{2}}x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+...+c_{1}e_{1}\right).

We claim that
r=\frac{a_{1}b_{1}}{k^{2}}x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+...+c_{1}e_{1}
is irreducible and quadratic in
x_{3}
. Otherwise, according to Corollary
2.14
, there must be an irreducible factor
r^{\prime}
of
r
which is linear in
x_{3}
hence we have a component
Z(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},r^{\prime},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
of
Case 3
by Definition
3.5
. Consider the proof of Corollary
4.4
we know
r^{\prime}=x_{3}-k^{\prime}x_{1}
or
x_{1}x_{3}-k^{\prime}
for some
k^{\prime}\neq 0
. By Lemma
4.3
, it must be
r^{\prime}=x_{3}-k^{\prime}x_{1}=x_{3}-kx_{1}
since we cannot have
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}
. In the meanwhile, the highest degrees of
x_{1},x_{3}
in
r
are at most 2. This leads to
a_{1}b_{1}=c_{1}e_{1}=0
given
r^{\prime}|r
hence
g^{(1)}
is either singular or reducible by Lemma
2.10
, a contradiction! So finally we have another component

(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},r,H^{(1)},H^{(2)})

with the extension diagram in which
[K_{1}(x_{3}):K_{1}]=2
(i.e.
x_{3}\notin K_{1}
). Notice that

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}\Rightarrow\left\{\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}\right\}.

Hence by Lemma
4.6
we know
W
is of
Case 4
. The proof is similar when
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}
holds but
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}\right\}
fails.
∎

4.2.5
Purely-quadratic

The proof of Theorem
4.1
is a direct consequence of Corollary
4.4
and Lemma
4.6
.

This class is currently "floating in the air" since we only proved its existence. In Theorem
4.1
when the condition of purely-quadratic couplings holds, we are expecting a component of
Case 4
with an explicit representation from equation (
8
)

W=Z(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},r_{k},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})

where
r_{k}(x_{1},x_{3})
is an irreducible factor of
\text{Res}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})
. In fact, for every component of
Case 4
, we have
r_{k}
in the form

r_{k}(x_{1},x_{3})=(\alpha_{22}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{02})x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{11}x_{1}x_{3}+(\alpha_{20}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{00}).

The proof can be found later in Corollary
6.6
where all
\alpha_{ij}
are given explicitly.

4.2.6
Involutive-quadratic

The proof of Theorem
4.1
is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.7
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})
, then
S
only admits components of
Case 5
if and only if system (
14
) holds. Moreover, the factorization of the resultant is

\text{Res}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})=\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}(a_{2}x_{1}x_{3}^{2}-(a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1})x_{3}+b_{2}x_{1})^{2}.

Remark 4.8
.

Under the condition of system (
14
), we have
a_{i}e_{i}=b_{i}c_{i}
for both
i=1,2
. In a real flexible linkage, this implies the so-called
orthodiagonal
property which is
\cos(\lambda_{i})\cos(\mu_{i})=\cos(\gamma_{i})\cos(\delta_{i})
.

4.2.7
Quartic

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
Suppose
S
does not satisfy the requirements of other classes. In particular, both
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible. Firstly,
S
does not have components of
Case 1
or
Case 2
which requires reducibility on
g^{(i)}
. Secondly,
S
does not have components of
Case 3
or
Case 4
by Corollary
4.4
and Lemma
4.6
. Finally,
S
does not have components of
Case 5
by Lemma
4.7
. Thus
S
only admits component of
Case 6
.
∎

5
General type without (anti)deltoids

5.1
Section outline and classification theorem

In this section, we study the couplings from matchings of general type in which not all
F_{i}\in\{0,\infty\}
. So, up to a rotation, we may always assume
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
. We follow our approach from pseudo-planar type and make a case-by-case study. It is interesting to mention that condition
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
implies even more restrictions on coefficients compared to pseudo-planar type. One will see that involutive-rational, rational-quadratic, and purely-quadratic couplings of general type are completely useless for the construction of flexible meshes. So, to avoid unnecessary discussion, we will treat them all together as equimodular class (see Definition
3.6
).

Theorem 5.1
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
where
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
. According to Definition
3.6
, each class of
S
is equivalent to one of the corresponding conditions. i.e.
S
is

\bullet

purely-rational
if both
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are reducible;

\bullet

half-quadratic
if only one of
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
is reducible;

Further, when both
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible,
S
is

\bullet

equimodular
if

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{e_{1}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{1}},\frac{a_{2}}{e_{2}}=\frac{c_{2}}{b_{2}},F_{1}=\pm 1,\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}-8a_{1}c_{1}}{16a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{16a_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}-8a_{2}b_{2}}\right\};

(18)

\bullet

involutive-quadratic
if

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}=\frac{c_{1}}{e_{1}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{e_{2}}=-1,F_{1}\neq\pm 1\right\}\text{ or}\\
\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}=\frac{c_{1}}{e_{1}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{e_{2}}=-1,F_{1}=\pm 1,256a_{1}c_{1}a_{2}b_{2}\neq 1\right\};\end{array}\right.

(19)

\bullet

quartic
if otherwise.

The proof has been split into cases which will be detailed studied.

5.2
Case-by-case study

5.2.1
Purely-rational

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is left to the reader. In fact, the proof for purely-rational class and half-quadratic class can be directly copied from their counterparts in pseudo-planar type, Section
4
. This is because in the corresponding extension diagram,
[K_{i}(y_{j}):K_{i}]=[K_{i}(x_{j+1}):K_{i}]
for all
i,j
. Hence by Definition
3.5
, the component remains in the same
Case
no matter
F_{1}\in\{0,\infty\}
or not.

5.2.2
Half-quadratic

The proof of Theorem
5.1
was mentioned above. Nevertheless, given a couping
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
which admits a component
W
of
Case 2
, we know it is either
\tilde{g}^{(1)}
is reducible or
g^{(2)}
is reducible (but not both). So when
\tilde{g}^{(1)}
is reducible, in the extension diagram of
W
we have
x_{2}\in K_{1}
and the minimal polynomial of
y_{2}
on
K_{1}
can be obtained from
g^{(2)}
through substitution of
x_{2}=x_{2}(x_{1})
; similarly, when
g^{(2)}
is reducible we have
x_{2}\in K_{3}=\mathbb{C}(y_{2})
and the minimal polynomial of
y_{2}
on
K_{1}
can be obtained from
\tilde{g}^{(1)}
through substitution
x_{2}=x_{2}(y_{2})
.

5.2.3
Equimodular

As in pseudo-planar type, the problem can be reduced to identical discriminants up to a square in
K_{2}
. To start up, fix an extension diagram and change the base field to
K_{2}=\mathbb{C}(x_{2})=\mathbb{C}(y_{1})
so
x_{1},y_{2}
are algebraic elements on
K_{2}
. From
g^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(1)}
, and
g^{(2)}
respectively, we have discriminants

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}=-4a_{1}c_{1}y_{1}^{4}+(1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1})y_{1}^{2}-4b_{1}e_{1},\\
\Delta_{1}=-4a_{1}c_{1}(x_{2}+F_{1})^{4}+(1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1})(x_{2}+F_{1})^{2}(1-F_{1}x_{2})^{2}-4b_{1}e_{1}(1-F_{1}x_{2})^{4},\\
\Delta_{2}=-4a_{2}b_{2}x_{2}^{4}+(1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2})x_{2}^{2}-4c_{2}e_{2}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(20)

Knowing that
H^{(1)}=0
,
\Delta_{1}
is actually obtained from
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
by substitution
y_{1}=\frac{x_{2}+F_{1}}{1-F_{1}x_{2}}
.

We skip the proof of the following lemma since it is a complete analog of Lemma
4.6
from pseudo-planar type, however, it applies to both types.

Lemma 5.2
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
where
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible.
S
is equimodular if and only if in every extension diagram of its components, one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(1)

y_{2}\in K_{1}(x_{2})
; (2)
y_{2}\in K_{2}(x_{1})
; (3)
x_{1}\in K_{2}(y_{2})
; (4)
x_{1}\in K_{3}(x_{2})
.

On the other hand, regarding
\tilde{g}^{(1)}(t,x_{2}),g^{(2)}(x_{2},t)
as polynomials in
K_{2}[t]
with discriminants
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
respectively,
S
is equimodular if and only if
\exists f\in K_{2}
such that
f^{2}=\frac{\Delta_{1}}{\Delta_{2}}
.

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
where
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible and
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
.
S
is equimodular if and only if system (
18
) holds.

When
\tilde{g}^{(1)}
is irreducible, it determines the minimal polynomial of
x_{2}
on
K_{1}
in every component. It is convenient to use the expression

\tilde{g}^{(1)}=h_{2}(x_{1})x_{2}^{2}+h_{1}(x_{1})x_{2}+h_{0}(x_{1})

where

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}h_{2}(x_{1})=(b_{1}F_{1}^{2}+a_{1})x_{1}^{2}-F_{1}x_{1}+e_{1}F_{1}^{2}+c_{1},\\
h_{1}(x_{1})=2F_{1}(a_{1}-b_{1})x_{1}^{2}+(1-F_{1}^{2})x_{1}+2F_{1}(c_{1}-e_{1}),\\
h_{0}(x_{1})=(a_{1}F_{1}^{2}+b_{1})x_{1}^{2}+F_{1}x_{1}+c_{1}F_{1}^{2}+e_{1}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(21)

Now let us suppose the component is of
Case 3
with the extension diagram in which
y_{2}\in K_{1}
, we have

Lemma 5.4
.

If a non-singular coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
admits a component of
Case 1
or
Case 3
, then the relation between
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
in the extension diagram must be in the form
y_{2}=\frac{px_{1}+q}{rx_{1}+s},ps-qr\neq 0
.

Definition 5.5
.

For a component
W
of a coupling, we say
W
is
real
if
W\cap(\mathbb{R}P^{1})^{5}
is an infinite set.

A surprising fact about equimodular couplings of general type is that they have no real components.

Proposition 5.6
.

An equimodular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
admits a real component only if
F_{1}\in\{0,\infty\}
and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}>0\text{ if }F_{1}=0,\\
\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}>0\text{ if }F_{1}=\infty.\\
\end{array}\right.

(22)

The above proposition tells us, in reality, any two spherical quads as coupled in Fig.
3
left can never determine an equimodular coupling of general type.

5.2.4
Involutive-quadratic

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is a consequence of a stronger version of Lemma
4.7
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Given a non-singular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
where
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
, then
S
only admits components of
Case 5
if and only if one of systems (
19
) holds. Moreover, the factorization of the resultant is

\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})=-(a_{2}h_{1}(x_{1})y_{2}^{2}-h_{2}(x_{1})y_{2}+b_{2}h_{1}(x_{1}))^{2}

where
h_{i}(x_{1})
are given in equation (
21
).

Remark 5.8
.

The conditions in Lemma
5.7
impose restrictions on the shape of the quads, i.e.

\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}=\frac{c_{1}}{e_{1}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{e_{2}}=-1\Leftrightarrow\delta_{1}=\mu_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\mu_{2}=\frac{\pi}{2}.

5.2.5
Quartic

Considering
\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}
are irreducible, the proof of Theorem
5.1
is a consequence of Lemma
5.3
and
5.7
.

6
Resultant analysis

6.1
Section outline and factorization theorem

For a given coupling, the following theorem describes its class in a different way. This new perspective is very important for the construction of matchings (i.e. flexible meshes) using Theorem
3.3
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Depending on the class of a non-singular coupling
S=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
, the factorization of
R_{1}:=\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)};x_{2})
is as follows:

\bullet

purely-rational
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(r_{i}x_{1}y_{2}-p_{i}x_{1}+s_{i}y_{2}-q_{i})
;

\bullet

half-quadratic
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=r(x_{1},y_{2})\cdot r^{\prime}(x_{1},y_{2})
where
r,r^{\prime}
are irreducible and quadratic in both
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
;

\bullet

involutive-rational
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=\prod_{i=1}^{2}(r_{i}x_{1}y_{2}-p_{i}x_{1}+s_{i}y_{2}-q_{i})^{2}
;

\bullet

rational-quadratic
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=(r_{1}x_{1}y_{2}-p_{1}x_{1}+s_{1}y_{2}-q_{1})^{2}\cdot r(x_{1},y_{2})
where
r
is irreducible and quadratic in both
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
;

\bullet

purely-quadratic
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=r(x_{1},y_{2})\cdot r^{\prime}(x_{1},y_{2})
where
r,r^{\prime}
are irreducible and quadratic in both
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
;

\bullet

involutive-quadratic
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})=\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}(a_{2}h_{1}(x_{1})y_{2}^{2}-h_{2}(x_{1})y_{2}+b_{2}h_{1}(x_{1}))^{2}
where
h_{i}(x_{1})
are the coefficients of
x_{2}^{i}
in
\tilde{g}^{(1)}
for
i=0,1,2
respectively;

\bullet

quartic
:
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})
is irreducible.

The proof will be given in the next section where
R_{1}
is factorized explicitly.

6.2
Symmetric Extension Theorem

Lemma 6.2
.

In the extension diagram of a component of a non-singular coupling, consider switching the parameter between
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
,
x_{3}
can be rationally expressed by
x_{1}
if and only if
x_{1}
can be rationally expressed by
x_{3}
.

Proof.

One direction has already been proved in Lemma
5.4
. For the other direction, carefully check the proof of Lemma
5.4
, one will find that if we alternate
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)})
to
(\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(1)})
and consider the problem in
K_{3}
, all arguments are still valid. Thus
x_{3}
is rational in
x_{1}
if and only if
x_{1}
is rational in
x_{3}
.
∎

Theorem 6.3

(Symmetric Extension)
.

In the extension diagram of a component of a non-singular coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
,
[K_{i}(x_{j}):K_{i}]=[K_{j}(x_{i}):K_{j}]
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 3
. In addition, the extension diagrams with respect to
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
always belong to the same
Case
of Definition
3.5
.

The last statement is a bit subtle and can be regarded as a generalization of Lemma
6.2
. In other words, no matter how we interchange the parameters between
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
, so the extension diagram might be upside down, the extension degrees always fit the same
Case
of Definition
3.5
.

Corollary 6.4
.

For a non-singular coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
, every factor of
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})
(or
\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)};x_{2})
) has the same degree in
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
(or
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
).

Proposition 6.5
.

For a non-singular coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
,
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})
is always in 4th degree of
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
.

Recall Proposition
2.15
, each component is related to a factor
r
of
R_{1}
. The degree of
y_{2}
in
r
reflects the extension degree
[K_{1}(y_{2}):K_{1}]
in Definition
3.5
. According to the component types that a coupling contains, the factorization of
R_{1}
therefore becomes very predictable thanks to Corollary
6.4
and Proposition
6.5
.

Proof.

(Theorem
6.1
)
Firstly, according to table (
3
) and Corollary
6.4
, the component types explain the degree of
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
in each irreducible factor of
R_{1}
. In the light of Proposition
6.5
, the irreducibility of the factors given in Theorem
6.1
becomes obvious. The only thing left is to verify if
R_{1}
can be factorized as the theorem claimed. For efficiency, we only provide proper reparametrizations so that the symbolic computation can factorize
R_{1}
explicitly.

\bullet

purely-rational
or
half-quadratic
: Keep the original expression of
g^{(i)}
if irreducible, otherwise use the expressions in Lemma
2.10
.

\bullet

involutive-rational
or
rational-quadratic
: Set
F_{1}=0
, check the proof of Theorem
4.1
for these two classes and replace
x_{3}
by
y_{2}
. The proof is similar for
F_{1}=\infty
. It is unnecessary to consider the general type
F_{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}
due to Proposition
5.6
. However, the factorizations stated in Theorem
6.1
still hold for general type (see Appendix
B
).

\bullet

purely-quadratic
: Given table (
3
) and Proposition
6.5
, it is clear that
R_{1}=rr^{\prime}
where
r,r^{\prime}
are irreducible and quadratic in both
x_{1}
and
y_{2}
. However, an explicit factorization is only useful with the restrictions in Proposition
5.6
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
F_{1}=0
and

\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}=m^{2}>0\Rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}a_{1}=\frac{b_{1}b_{2}(4b_{2}c_{2}m^{2}-4b_{1}c_{1}-m^{2}+1)}{4e_{2}(b_{2}c_{2}m^{2}-b_{1}c_{1})},\\
e_{1}=\frac{c_{2}e_{2}m^{2}}{b_{1}},\\
a_{2}=\frac{b_{1}c_{1}(4b_{2}c_{2}m^{2}-4b_{1}c_{1}-m^{2}+1)}{4e_{2}m^{2}(b_{2}c_{2}m^{2}-b_{1}c_{1})}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(23)

According to Corollary
2.11
,
\{a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},e_{i}\}
includes at most one zero for
i=1,2
. In addition, given
\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}>0
, the zeros, if exist, must gather in one fraction. Hence it is harmless to assume
b_{1}c_{1}e_{1}b_{2}c_{2}e_{2}\neq 0
so
a_{1},e_{1},a_{2}
can be parametrized as in equation (
23
). Nevertheless, we should mention
b_{2}c_{2}m^{2}-b_{1}c_{1}\neq 0
. Otherwise

m^{2}=\frac{b_{1}c_{1}}{b_{2}c_{2}}\Rightarrow\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}},\frac{c_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{e_{1}}{e_{2}}\right\}\Rightarrow m^{2}=\frac{a_{1}e_{1}}{a_{2}e_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}c_{1}}{b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=1,

contradicts to systems (
13
).

\bullet

involutive-quadratic
: Check the counterparts of the proof of Theorem
4.1
and
5.1
.

\bullet

quartic
: Trivial.

∎

Corollary 6.6
.

Suppose coupling
S
is rational-quadratic or purely-quadratic. If
S
admits a real component (Definition
5.5
) of
Case 4
, the quadratic factors
r,r^{\prime}
of
R_{1}
in Theorem
6.1
are in the form

(\alpha_{22}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{02})y_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{11}x_{1}y_{2}+(\alpha_{20}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{00})

where
\alpha_{ij}
are given in table (
24
).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
F_{1}=0
due to Proposition
5.6
. Using the parametrizations as suggested in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, symbolic computation immediately shows table (
24
) for rational-quadratic and purely-quadratic couplings respectively.
∎

rational-quadratic

rational-quadratic

purely-quadratic

\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}=k

\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k}

\frac{a_{1}c_{1}}{a_{2}b_{2}}=\frac{1-4a_{1}e_{1}-4b_{1}c_{1}}{1-4a_{2}e_{2}-4b_{2}c_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}e_{1}}{c_{2}e_{2}}=m^{2}

\alpha_{22}

\frac{2(a_{1}e_{1}-b_{1}c_{1})^{2}-a_{1}e_{1}-b_{1}c_{1}}{k}

(24)

7
Back to the mesh

7.1
Section outline and the classification of flexible meshes

As one already knew, each flexible mesh uniquely determines a matching
M=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)})
hence the mesh can be classified by its corresponding matching. According to Proposition
2.15
,
M
can be regarded as the sum of two couplings

S_{1}=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)}),\,\,S_{2}=(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)},H^{(3)},H^{(4)}).

And by Theorem
3.3
, we must have
\gcd(\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)};x_{2}),\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)};x_{4}))\neq 1
. Hence a very straightforward classification can be given by the factors of the gcd. To achieve this, Theorem
6.1
is a powerful tool. Notice that in the extension diagram of any component, the minimal polynomial of
x_{3}
on
K_{1}
can be induced by the minimal polynomial of
y_{2}
on
K_{1}
through substitution
y_{2}=\frac{x_{3}+F_{2}}{1-F_{2}x_{3}}
. Consequently, the factorization of
\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)};x_{2})
can be induced by the factorization of
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})
through the same substitution. Thus by Theorem
6.1
and Corollary
6.4
, the classification of flexible meshes can be naturally given as below.

Definition 7.1
.

Given a flexible mesh that determines a non-singular matching

M:=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}),

the corresponding couplings are

S_{1}:=(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},H^{(1)},H^{(2)}),\,\,S_{2}:=(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)},H^{(3)},H^{(4)}).

Set

\tilde{R}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{3}):=\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)};x_{2}),\,\,\tilde{R}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{3}):=\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)};x_{4}),\,\,d_{M}:=\gcd(\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}).

We say
M
(or the mesh) is

\bullet

PR
if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are purely-rational so
d_{M}
has linear factors only;

\bullet

HQ
if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are half-quadratic so
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only;

\bullet

IR
if for
i=1,2
,
S_{i}
is either involutive-rational or rational-quadratic and
d_{M}
has linear factors only;

\bullet

RQ
if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are rational-quadratic and
d_{M}
has linear and quadratic factors;

\bullet

PQ
if for
i=1,2
,
S_{i}
is either rational-quadratic or purely-quadratic and
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only;

\bullet

IQ
if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are involutive-quadratic so
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only;

\bullet

Q
if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are quartic so
d_{M}=\tilde{R}^{(1)}
is irreducible;

\bullet

PR + IR
if one of
S_{i}
is purely-rational and the other is either involutive-rational or rational-quadratic so
d_{M}
has linear factors only;

\bullet

HQ + IQ
if one of
S_{i}
is half-quadratic and the other is involutive-quadratic so
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only;

\bullet

HQ + PQ
if one of
S_{i}
is half-quadratic and the other is either rational-quadratic or purely-quadratic so
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only;

\bullet

PQ + IQ
if one of
S_{i}
is involutive-quadratic and the other is either rational-quadratic or purely-quadratic so
d_{M}
has quadratic factors only.

The above definition just proved Theorem
1.1
, and in the rest of this section,
M,S_{1},S_{2},\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
, and
d_{M}
always stay their meanings from Definition
7.1
.

7.2
General construction of matchings

The construction of matchings is equivalent to solving a polynomial system on

\{a_{3},b_{3},c_{3},e_{3},a_{4},b_{4},c_{4},e_{4},F_{2},F_{3},F_{4}\}.

(25)

For example, we arbitrarily set
\{\tilde{g}^{(1)},g^{(2)}\}
, as known as

\{a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},e_{1},a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},e_{2},F_{1}\},

then we have an explicit factorization of
R_{1}(x_{1},y_{2})
by Theorem
6.1
. Therefore we have the factorization of
\tilde{R}^{(1)}
with
F_{2}
as a parameter in it. On the other hand, to construct a specific matching, we need to settle down the class of
S_{2}
so that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
can form a valid combination in Definition
7.1
. And once we know the class of
S_{2}
, more restrictions from Theorem
4.1
or
5.1
will be added on

\{a_{3},b_{3},c_{3},e_{3},a_{4},b_{4},c_{4},e_{4},F_{3},F_{4}\}.

In the meanwhile, Theorem
6.1
also allows us to factorize
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
symbolically. Finally, given
d_{M}\neq 1
, there must be two irreducible factors of
\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
respectively such that their coefficients of
x_{1}^{i}x_{3}^{j}
are proportional hence we get the desired polynomial system on (
25
).

A matching
M
always comes with an infinite zero set
Z(M)\cong Z(S_{1})\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}Z(S_{2})
and the essential reason for
Z(M)
being infinite is that there are components
W_{1},W_{2}
of
Z(S_{1}),Z(S_{2})
respectively such that
W_{1}\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}W_{2}
is infinite. It is easy to see that the first seven classes from
PR
to
Q
in Definition
7.1
require
W_{1},W_{2}
belonging to the same
Case
of Definition
3.5
, hence we call them
simple classes
. On the contrary, the rest from
PR + IR
to
PQ + IQ
are called
hybrid classes
since
W_{1},W_{2}
are required to be of different
Cases
. In this regard,

Definition 7.2
.

Given a matching
M
with corresponding couplings
S_{1},S_{2}
. A component
W_{i}
of
S_{i}
is said to be
valid
if for
j\neq i
, there exists a component
W_{j}
of
S_{j}
such that
W_{i}\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}W_{j}
is an infinite set.

7.3
Simple classes

7.3.1
PR:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 1
.

In such a class both
S_{1},S_{2}
are purely-rational so all
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
and
g^{(i)}
are reducible. The name ’purely-rational’ is based on the fact that in the extension diagram of every component of
S_{i}
, all field extensions are trivial. Geometrically speaking, all forming quads in the spherical linkage of a
PR
mesh are (anti)isogonal (Corollary
2.11
). This means all
PR
meshes are just those we described in Theorem
3.7
. For illustration, we provide an algebraic construction when all quads are antiisogonal. In other words, all
PR
matchings
M
in which
a_{i}=e_{i}=0
,
\forall i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
(Lemma
2.5
), can be constructed as below.

Example 7.3
.

Here we construct matchings
(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)})
in which
a_{i}=e_{i}=0
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. First, set

\{b_{1}\neq 0,c_{1}\neq 0,b_{2}\neq 0,c_{2}\neq 0,b_{3}\neq 0,c_{3}\neq 0,c_{4}\neq 0,F_{1},F_{2}\}

as free parameters and

\{b_{4}\neq 0,F_{3},F_{4}\}

undetermined. Following the proof of Theorem
3.7
we have

N_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}k_{i}&-F_{i}\\
k_{i}F_{i}&1\\
\end{array}\right)\text{ or }N_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
k_{i}&0\\
\end{array}\right)

where
k_{i}=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{1-4b_{i}c_{i}}}{2c_{i}}\neq 0
. Since any scalar matrix is the identity of Möbius transformation,

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n&0\\
0&n\\
\end{array}\right)(x)=\frac{nx+0}{0x+n}=x,\forall n\neq 0,

we may regard
N_{i}\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
hence the inverse of a matrix, despite a scalar, is simply its adjoint matrix. Simple observation shows

\begin{array}[]{l}N_{i}\in T:=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{array}\right)\in\text{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R}):ab+cd=0\right\},\\
N_{i}^{-1}\in T^{\prime}:=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{array}\right)\in\text{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R}):ac+bd=0\right\},\\
\end{array}

which implies
N_{3}N_{2}N_{1}\in T^{\prime}
since
N_{4}N_{3}N_{2}N_{1}
is a scalar. Notice that the map

f:\,\,\mathbb{R}P^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}P^{1};\,\,x\mapsto\frac{2x}{1-x^{2}}

is onto given
f(\tan(\alpha))=\tan(2\alpha)
. Hence
\exists F_{3}\in\mathbb{R}P^{1}
such that

\frac{2F_{3}}{1-F_{3}^{2}}=\frac{2(ac+bd)}{c^{2}+d^{2}-a^{2}-b^{2}}\text{ where }\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{array}\right):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}k_{3}&0\\
0&1\\
\end{array}\right)N_{2}N_{1}.

This is equivalent to saying that
\exists F_{3}\in\mathbb{R}P^{1}
such that

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}\\
c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right):=N_{3}N_{2}N_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-F_{3}\\
F_{3}&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{array}\right)\in T^{\prime},\text{ i.e. }a^{\prime}c^{\prime}+b^{\prime}d^{\prime}=0,

so we must have
a^{\prime}\neq 0
or
b^{\prime}\neq 0
. Thus in
PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
we have

N_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}d^{\prime}&-b^{\prime}\\
-c^{\prime}&a^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{d^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}}&-\frac{b^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}}\\
-\frac{c^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}}&1\\
\end{array}\right)\text{ or }N_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
-\frac{c^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}&0\\
\end{array}\right).

i.e.
(k_{4},F_{4})=(\frac{d^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}},\frac{b^{\prime}}{a^{\prime}})
or
(-\frac{c^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}},\infty)
. Finally, although
c_{4}
was mentioned to be free, we need to make sure
c_{4}k_{4}\neq-1
so that
b_{4}=-c_{4}k_{4}^{2}-k_{4}\neq 0
(recall
k_{i}=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{1-4b_{i}c_{i}}}{2c_{i}}
).

Remark 7.4
.

For each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, a
PR
mesh requires either
a_{i}=e_{i}=0
or
b_{i}=c_{i}=0
. However, one should realize that the above example is typical. The approach can be adapted to any other case after a minor change.

7.3.2
HQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 2
.

In such a class both
S_{1},S_{2}
are half-quadratic so each of them only contains one reducible
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
. The extension diagram of a valid component of
S_{1}
leads to either
x_{2}\in K_{1}
or
x_{2}\in K_{3}
but not both. That is why we call it ’half-quadratic’. Please note that we only need to consider the matchings in which
\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(3)}
are reducible or
\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}
are reducible. This is because when reducible polynomials are adjacent, say
\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}
, then write the matching as
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)})
, it goes to
PR + IR
class.

Example
4.2
gave a way to construct pseudo-planar
HQ
matchings in which
\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(3)}
are reducible. Clearly, after a rotation, it also works for those in which
\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}
are reducible.

7.3.3
IR:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 3
.

In such a class all
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
are irreducible and
d_{M}
has linear factors only. This means in the extension diagram of any valid component we have
x_{3}\in K_{1}
. So
\tilde{g}^{(1)}(x_{1},t),\tilde{g}^{(2)}(t,x_{3})\in K_{1}[t]
determine the same minimal polynomial of
x_{2}
on
K_{1}
which means they have two common roots in
K_{1}(x_{2})
. Same thing happens on
(\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)})
and
x_{4}
. The name ’involutive-rational’ therefore comes from here.

Example
4.5
showed how to construct pseudo-planar matchings
M
in which
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 3
. However, if both
S_{1},S_{2}
are rational-quadratic, we only need to adjust a few coefficients such that
\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
only coincide on their linear factors so
S_{i}
does not have valid components of
Case 4
.

As for matchings in general type, notice that real components of
Case 3
only exist when
F_{1},F_{3}\in\{0,\infty\}
(see Proposition
5.6
). Suppose
W_{1},W_{2}
are real valid components of
S_{1},S_{2}
respectively such that
W_{1}\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}W_{2}
is an infinite set. According to Theorem
3.3
, we are allowed to merge the extension diagrams

\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(2)}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(y_{2})=K_{1}(x_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(3)}}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(4)}}

According to the proof of Corollary
4.4
, we have
y_{2}=kx_{1}
or
y_{2}=k/x_{1}
. After substitutions
y_{4}=\frac{F_{4}+x_{1}}{1-F_{4}x_{1}}
and
x_{3}=\frac{y_{2}-F_{2}}{1+F_{2}y_{2}}
, the relation between
(y_{4},x_{3})
should keep the same format like
(y_{2},x_{1})
. This immediately shows the following restrictions.

\{F_{2}F_{4}=k=\pm 1\}\cup\{-F_{2}/F_{4}=k=\pm 1\}\cup\{-F_{2}F_{4}=k=\pm 1\}\cup\{F_{2}/F_{4}=k=\pm 1\}.

(26)

7.3.4
RQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 3
and
Case 4
.

In such a class
\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
have the same factorization: an irreducible and quadratic factor times a squared linear factor. This explains the name ’ration-quadratic’. We only need to consider pseudo-planar type because of the following proposition.

Proposition 7.5
.

A matching
M
must be pseudo-planar if, for
i=1,2
, coupling
S_{i}
admits a valid component
W_{i}
of
Case 4
such that

(W_{1}\cap(\mathbb{R}P^{1})^{5})\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}(W_{2}\cap(\mathbb{R}P^{1})^{5})

is an infinite set.

Proof.

Clearly,
W_{1},W_{2}
are real components of
S_{1},S_{2}
respectively, hence
F_{1},F_{3}\in\{0,\infty\}
by Proposition
5.6
. Moreover, due to Theorem
3.3
, we can merge the extension diagrams

\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(4)}}

(27)

where
K_{1}(x_{2})=K_{1}(x_{3})=K_{1}(x_{4})
is based on Lemma
5.2
. The same lemma also tells coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},H^{(4)},H^{(1)})
admits a real component of
Case 3
or
Case 4
. And again, by Proposition
5.6
we have
F_{4}\in\{0,\infty\}
. Finally, thanks to Theorem
6.3
we can use the same argument to conclude
F_{2}\in\{0,\infty\}
. Thus the whole matching must be pseudo-planar.
∎

The construction is easy. First, we use Example
4.5
to get some candidates so
\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
share at least a linear factor. Then use Corollary
6.6
to get the quadratic factors and make the corresponding coefficients proportional.

7.3.5
PQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 4
.

In such a class we can always get a merged diagram (
27
) in which

K_{1}(x_{2})=K_{1}(x_{3})=K_{1}(x_{4})=K_{1}(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\text{ and }[K_{1}(x_{2}):K_{1}]=2.

The name ’purely-quadratic’ is quite straightforward.

The construction is basically as suggested in Section
7.2
. However, given
M
is pseudo-planar (Proposition
7.5
), and according to Corollary
6.6
, the polynomial system is reduced to four equations which are derived from proportional coefficients
\alpha_{ij}
. But do not forget to remove the solutions of
RQ
class when both
S_{1},S_{2}
are wanted to be rational-quadratic.

7.3.6
IQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 5
.

Similar to
IR
matchings,
\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)}
determine the same minimal polynomial of
x_{2}
on
K_{1}(x_{3})
and
[K_{1}(x_{3}),K_{1}]=2
. So it is called ’involutive-quadratic’. This class belongs to the so-called
orthodiagonal involutive type
which is well studied by Aikyn in
[
35
]
. They gave a systematic construction for such matchings and found the equivalent condition for the valid components being real. However, we have an important result for pseudo-planar
IQ
matchings.

Proposition 7.6
.

In a pseudo-planar matching
M
with couplings
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
,
S_{1}
is involutive-quadratic if and only if
S_{2}
is involutive-quadratic.

Proof.

Suppose
S_{i}
is involutive-quadratic so its valid component must be of
Case 5
. From Lemma
4.7
we know that the irreducible factor of
\tilde{R}^{(i)}
is quadratic in both
x_{1},x_{3}
and only contains odd-degree terms. On the contrary, going through pseudo-planar couplings of other classes, the irreducible factors of the resultant, if quadratic, only contain even-degree terms (see Example
4.2
and Corollary
6.6
). Hence the other coupling must be involutive-quadratic as well.
∎

7.3.7
Q:
S_{1},S_{2}
only admit valid components of
Case 6
.

This is the irreducible class for
\tilde{R}^{(1)},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
, so in order to form a matching, we must have
\tilde{R}^{(1)}=c\tilde{R}^{(2)}
for a constant
c
. We call it ’quartic’ simply because all components of the couplings lead to
[K_{1}(x_{3}):K_{1}]=4
.

Again, the construction is as suggested in Section
7.2
and Example
3.8
(Reflection) shows the solution exists.
In addition, it is easy to verify that by relabeling the quads in Example
2.2
, e.g. considering couplings
(\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)},H^{(2)},H^{(3)})
and
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},H^{(4)},H^{(1)})
, one may obtain a non-reflection
Q
mesh.

Hellmuth Stachel conjectured a long time ago that there do not exist irreducible matchings. In our language, in a flexible mesh which determines a matching
M
,
\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)};x_{2})
is irreducible only if
\text{Res}(\tilde{g}^{(2)},\tilde{g}^{(3)};x_{3})
is reducible. Or
[K_{1}(x_{3}):K_{1}]=4\Rightarrow[K_{2}(x_{4}):K_{2}]\leq 2
for short. The planar version
11
11

11

All forming panels in Fig.
1
left are planar.
of the conjecture has been proved by Erofeev in
[
36
]
. If the conjecture is also true for non-planar type, the construction for
Q
matchings can be transformed into other classes after a rotation.

7.4
Hybrid classes

7.4.1
PR + IR:
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 1
and
Case 3
respectively, or vice versa.

The construction for this class is just a byproduct of Example
7.3
. Symmetrically, we assume
S_{1}
admits a valid component of
Case 3
hence
S_{2}
is purely-rational. In addition, we may assume
F_{1}\in\{0,\infty\}
due to Proposition
5.6
.

Example 7.7
.

We construct matchings
M
in which
F_{1}=0
and the corresponding couplings
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components
W_{1},W_{2}
of
Case 3
and
Case 1
respectively. i.e.
\tilde{g}^{(1)},\tilde{g}^{(2)}
are irreducible and
\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}
are reducible. According to Theorem
3.3
, the merged extension diagram of
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
is

\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2},y_{2})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{(2)}}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(1)}}

Since
F_{1}=0
, from Corollary
4.4
and its proof, we know that in the diagram it is either
y_{2}=kx_{1}
or
y_{2}=k/x_{1}
, depending on whether the coefficients of
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
satisfy

\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{c_{2}}=\frac{b_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}=k\right\}\text{ or }\left\{\frac{a_{1}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}}{c_{1}}=\frac{c_{2}}{e_{1}}=\frac{1}{k}\right\}

where
k\neq 0
is a constant. In either of the above, the relation between
(x_{1},x_{3})
is a Möbius transformation
x_{3}=\frac{px_{1}+q}{rx_{1}+s}
where
p,q,r,s
are parameterized by
k
and
F_{2}
. The rest construction for
\tilde{g}^{(3)},\tilde{g}^{(4)}
goes the same way as in Theorem
3.7
or Example
7.3
, i.e.

N_{4}N_{3}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p&q\\
r&s\\
\end{array}\right)

must be a scalar matrix.

7.4.2
HQ + IQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 2
and
Case 5
respectively, or vice versa.

The construction is as suggested in Section
7.2
. Nevertheless, we want to mention that, it is easy to realize one of
S_{i}
is half-quadratic and the other is involutive-quadratic hence there is only one
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
reducible. We may temporarily assume it is
\tilde{g}^{(2)}
so there are valid components
W_{1},W_{2}
of
S_{1},S_{2}
respectively such that
W_{1}\times_{\{x_{1},x_{3}\}}W_{2}
is an infinite set. The following diagrams are from the same merged diagram of
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
but with respect to different parameters
x_{1},x_{4}
respectively.

\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}(x_{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(3)}}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(4)}}
\textstyle{K_{4}(x_{1},x_{2})}
\textstyle{K_{4}(x_{2},x_{3})}
\textstyle{K_{4}(x_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(1)}}
\textstyle{K_{4}(x_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{4}(x_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(4)}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}^{(3)}}

According to Definition
3.5
we have
K_{1}(x_{2})=K_{1}(x_{3})\neq K_{1}(x_{4})
and
[K_{4}(x_{2}):K_{4}]=[K_{4}(x_{3}):K_{4}]=2
. So by Lemma
5.2
, coupling
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},H^{(4)},H^{(1)})
, like
S_{2}
, also admits a valid component of
Case 5
. This means no matter how we label the polynomials
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
, an
HQ + IQ
matching is always an
HQ + IQ
matching. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can always assume
\tilde{g}^{(2)}
is reducible and both couplings
S_{2}
and
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},H^{(4)},H^{(1)})
are involutive-quadratic. Hence by Theorem
4.1
or
5.1
we have

\left\{\frac{a_{4}}{b_{4}}=\frac{c_{4}}{e_{4}}=\frac{a_{1}}{c_{1}}=\frac{b_{1}}{e_{1}}(=-1),\frac{a_{3}}{b_{3}}=\frac{c_{3}}{e_{3}}=\frac{a_{4}}{c_{4}}=\frac{b_{4}}{e_{4}}(=-1)\right\}.

Adding this to the construction process will considerably reduce the number of parameters.
The solution exists according to the following example.

Example 7.8
.

We first set

b_{1}=-a_{1}\neq 0,e_{1}=-c_{1}\neq 0,a_{2}=e_{2}=0,b_{2}=-c_{2}-1,b_{2}c_{2}\neq 0,F_{1}=1,F_{2}=0

plus the inequalities in Definition
2.6
. Simple calculation shows
\tilde{R}^{(1)}
has an irreducible factor

x_{1}x_{3}^{2}-4(a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1})x_{3}^{2}-x_{1}

which is also wanted to be a factor of
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
. So finally, let
F_{3}=F_{4}=0
and use Lemma
4.7
to construct
g^{(3)}
and
g^{(4)}
.

7.4.3
HQ + PQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 2
and
Case 4
respectively, or vice versa.

As we did for the last class, a similar argument can show that, no matter how we label the polynomials
\tilde{g}^{(i)}
, an
HQ + PQ
matching is always an
HQ + PQ
matching. Without loss of generality, we can always assume
\tilde{g}^{(2)}
is reducible and both couplings
S_{2}
and
(\tilde{g}^{(4)},\tilde{g}^{(1)},H^{(4)},H^{(1)})
admit valid components of
Case 4
. And due to Proposition
5.6
, we may further assume
F_{3},F_{4}\in\{0,\infty\}
. We first see an example of pseudo-planar type.

Example 7.9
.

We construct
HQ + PQ
matchings in which all
F_{i}=0
and
g^{(2)}
is reducible. Since
S_{2}
admits a valid component of
Case 4
, from Theorem
4.1

g^{(3)}
and
g^{(4)}
could be constructed by the first one of systems (
13
). Hence by Corollary
6.6
we get an irreducible factor of
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
explicitly

(\alpha_{22}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{02})x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{11}x_{1}x_{3}+(\alpha_{20}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{00}).

If necessary, we need to adjust a few coefficients to make sure
\alpha_{11}\neq 0
. On the other hand, by Lemma
2.10
, we arbitrarily set
g^{(2)}
reducible by letting
a_{2}=e_{2}=0
or
b_{2}=c_{2}=0
so
g^{(2)}
has a factor in the form
y_{2}-kx_{2}
or
x_{2}y_{2}-k
. We first consider the case
(y_{2}-kx_{2})|g^{(2)}
. In the merged extension diagram of valid components of
S_{1},S_{2}
, the minimal polynomial of
x_{3}
on
K_{1}
can be induced by substitutions

g^{(1)}(x_{1},y_{1})=g^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})=g^{(1)}\left(x_{1},\frac{y_{2}}{k}\right)=g^{(1)}\left(x_{1},\frac{x_{3}}{k}\right)

so
\tilde{R}^{(1)}
has an irreducible factor

(a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+c_{1})x_{3}^{2}+kx_{1}x_{3}+k^{2}(b_{1}x_{1}^{2}+e_{1})

which should be the same factor of
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
, i.e.
g^{(1)}
is determined by

\frac{a_{1}}{\alpha_{22}}=\frac{c_{1}}{\alpha_{02}}=\frac{k}{\alpha_{11}}=\frac{k^{2}b_{1}}{\alpha_{20}}=\frac{k^{2}e_{1}}{\alpha_{00}}

where
\alpha_{11}\neq 0
, as required, is mandatory. The case for
(x_{2}y_{2}-k)|g^{(2)}
is similar.

Clearly, there is no difficulty in applying the above method to construct all pseudo-planar
HQ + PQ
matchings since all polynomials remain in the same form. In fact, Example
7.9
is even typical for general type. Notice that we have restricted
F_{3},F_{4}\in\{0,\infty\}
so for general type we only allow
F_{1},F_{2}\notin\{0,\infty\}
. Likewise, after
g^{(1)}(x_{1},y_{1})
going through three steps of substitutions

\left(y_{1}=\frac{x_{2}+F_{1}}{1-F_{1}x_{2}}\right)\rightarrow\left(x_{2}=\frac{y_{2}}{k}\text{ or }x_{2}=\frac{k}{y_{2}}\right)\rightarrow\left(y_{2}=\frac{x_{3}+F_{2}}{1-F_{2}x_{3}}\right),

we still get an irreducible polynomial in the form

(\alpha_{22}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{02})x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{11}x_{1}x_{3}+(\alpha_{20}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{00})

that can match the irreducible factor of
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
without odd-degree terms. This leads to the same consequence (
26
) as in
IR
class (replace
F_{4}
by
F_{1}
). The rest work goes the same as in Example
7.9
.

7.4.4
PQ + IQ:
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 4
and
Case 5
respectively, or vice versa.

The construction is as suggested in Section
7.2
. The solution exists according to the following example.

Example 7.10
.

Suppose
S_{1},S_{2}
admit valid components of
Case 4
and
Case 5
respectively hence we may assume
F_{1}=0
due to Proposition
5.6
. Let
F_{2}=0
and according to Corollary
6.6
we have an irreducible factor of
\tilde{R}^{(1)}

(\alpha_{22}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{02})x_{3}^{2}+\alpha_{11}x_{1}x_{3}+(\alpha_{20}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{00}).

On the other hand, set
F_{3}=\pm 1,F_{4}=0
we get an irreducible factor of
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
by Lemma
5.7

4a_{4}(a_{3}x_{3}^{2}+c_{3})x_{1}^{2}+x_{3}x_{1}+4b_{4}(a_{3}x_{3}^{2}+c_{3}).

Thus the coefficients of
g^{(1)},g^{(2)}
can be determined by equation (
23
) and

\frac{4a_{3}a_{4}}{\alpha_{22}}=\frac{4a_{3}b_{4}}{\alpha_{02}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{11}}=\frac{4c_{3}a_{4}}{\alpha_{20}}=\frac{4c_{3}b_{4}}{\alpha_{00}}.

We call back to Example
2.2
for a physical model.

8
Conclusion and future work

In this article, we developed an algebraic approach to classify the flexible Kokotsakis polyhedra without (anti)deltoids, also as known as non-singular
3\times 3
flexible meshes. Among all 11 different classes, most of them are provided with systematic constructions, and for the rest, partial examples are given to demonstrate no empty class.

Obviously, the future work is to continue on singular ones which contain (anti)deltoids. Based on the results we obtained so far, our algebraic method still works but in an asymmetric way, unlike non-singular cases. On the other hand, the singularity may occur in many different ways hence a lot of reduction efforts are needed to provide a classification as concise as possible.

Appendix A
Definition of the dihedral angles

Following Fig.
1
right, we need to give a proper definition of dihedral angles
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
. To do that, we are about to relocate the starting points of all vectors to the center of a unit sphere. Let us consider a planar mesh that is partially depicted in Fig.
6
left.

Figure 6:
Notice that
(\lambda_{i},\gamma_{i},\mu_{i},\delta_{i})
and
(\lambda_{i}^{\prime},\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime},\delta_{i}^{\prime})
are complementary to
\pi
respectively.

\end{overpic}

Figure 7:
This figure is the decomposition of Fig.
6
right,
S
is the south pole.

In Fig.
6
right, we take the common orientation of the sphere where the surface normals are pointing outwards. And in Fig.
7
left, the oriented spherical quad
Q_{1}:(\lambda_{1},\delta_{1},\mu_{1},\gamma_{1})
determines its interior—the area which does not contain south pole
S
, hence the dihedral angles
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\in[0,2\pi)
are well defined by the interior angles of
Q_{1}
. However, if we adopt the same orientation on Fig.
7
right for
Q_{2}
, its interior would look strange—also the area which does not contain
S
. Therefore, in order to easily represent the relation between angles, we reverse the orientation and set
Q_{2}:(\lambda_{2},\gamma_{2},\mu_{2},\delta_{2})
, the dihedral angles
\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\in[0,2\pi)
are defined in the same way through the interior angles of
Q_{2}
.

In general, once we relocated all vectors to the sphere, the spherical quads are oriented as follows:

Q_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\lambda_{i},\delta_{i},\mu_{i},\gamma_{i}),\,\,i\in\{1,3\},\\
(\lambda_{i},\gamma_{i},\mu_{i},\delta_{i}),\,\,i\in\{2,4\}.\\
\end{array}\right.

The diheral angles
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
are hence defined by the corresponding interior angles of
Q_{i}
(notice that
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i+1}
).

When it comes to non-planar meshes (Fig.
2
), we keep the same orientation of the sphere and set
\boldsymbol{v_{i}}
be the surface normal at the common vertex of
Q_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
. Take Fig.
3
as an example, a neighborhood of
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}
can be stereographically projected (angle preserving) to its tangent plane (with the same orientation, see Fig.
8
).
